### Pair 236
- **question**: Why does the instrumentalist argue that people should treat state commands as authoritative reasons for obedience?
- **gold**: The instrumentalist offers two interconnected reasons. First, treating state commands as providing content-independent reasons for obedience is necessary for a reasonably just state to perform the tasks that make it just. If people constantly second-guess the state's decisions, the collective effects undermine the state's ability to coordinate, facilitate collective action, and provide assurance—all central to establishing justice. Second, individuals often fail to act in accordance with the reasons that actually apply to them when they do not treat state commands as authoritative, because only the state's commands can inform people about what rules establish justice in their particular community. Therefore, the instrumentalist concludes it is wrong for a person not to treat state commands as authoritative, at least in many circumstances.
- **model answer**:

  The instrumentalist argues that obedience to the state is necessary for justice because it provides content-independent reasons for acting justly. If people constantly second-guess whether the state's decisions are right, this undermines the coordination and assurance mechanisms essential for establishing justice within a society. The instrumentalist also contends that individuals will act less justly if they fail to recognize that the state commands offer such independent reasons because only the state can clue them into what rules establish justice in their community. This means obedience is not merely an instrument of justice but a necessary condition for its establishment, making it morally obligatory under certain circumstances.

The passage explains that if people were truly what they feel themselves to be (the "self-realization" Bradley refers to), then their actual existence would contradict the very idea of being a moral agent at all. The passage suggests that this raises a natural question about whether we can justify attributing understanding to aliens without sharing their biology—specifically asking how such attribution could be justified if the alien's biology is fundamentally different from ours.

### Pair 244
- **question**: What are the main limitations of Watson's question-answering abilities despite its success in Jeopardy!?
- **gold**: Watson struggles with two primary types of challenges beyond Jeopardy!-style quizzing. First, it cannot engage in on-the-fly conversation that requires intricate real-time reasoning rather than information retrieval from pre-existing data—such as resolving anaphora (pronoun references) or understanding commonsensical knowledge about time, space, history, and folk psychology. Second, Watson fails at dynamic question-answering, where answers may not exist in any recorded textual form at the time of questioning or where answers depend on factors that change over time. These limitations reveal that superior performance on factual retrieval tasks does not translate to the deeper reasoning capabilities needed for natural language understanding in more open-ended contexts.
- **model answer**:

  Watson excels at answering questions that can be answered by retrieving information from pre-existing data, but it fails to perform well on the kind of reasoning and commonsense understanding required for more complex tasks. The passage illustrates this with examples like anaphora resolution and other intricate reasoning problems that cannot be solved through simple retrieval-based methods. Additionally, Watson's performance is limited when questions require dynamic changes in context—such as answering a question about whether something is lucky based on the timing of events rather than pre-existing data. These limitations suggest that while Watson has achieved impressive results in Jeopardy!-style quizzing, it falls short for more sophisticated and dynamic reasoning tasks that involve genuine conversational engagement with the world around us.

### Pair 249
- **question**: What three constraints must qualitative preferences over prospects satisfy in order to guarantee that they maximize expected utility?
- **gold**: According to the expected utility hypothesis, qualitative preferences over prospects must satisfy three constraints: ordering, continuity, and independence. When these three conditions are met, the preferences will mathematically maximize expected utility as formalized by Neumann and Morgenstern. These constraints together ensure that a rational agent's decision-making framework is consistent with the expected utility principle established by Bernoulli.
- **model answer**:

  The qualitative preferences \(\succeq\) over prospects must satisfy the ordering constraint, which requires that if \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) are three alternatives such that \(a\succ b\) and \(b\succ c\), then \(a\succ c\). The continuity constraint demands that for any sequence of alternatives \(\{a_n\}\) with a limit point \(a\) in the set, if \(a_n\succeq a_{n+1}\) for all \(n\), then \(a_n\succeq a\) for all sufficiently large \(n\). The independence constraint requires that for any three alternatives \(\{a_i\}\), \(\{b_j\}\), and \(\{c_k\}\), if \(a_i\succ b_j\) and \(b_j\succ c_k\) for each \(i, j, k\), then the preference ordering among these alternatives is preserved: \(a_i\succ a_{i'}\) implies \(b_j\succ b_{j'}\) implies \(c_k\succ c_{k'}\) for any indices \(i'\) and \(j'\). Together, these three constraints—ordering, continuity, and independence—are sufficient to ensure that rational agents will maximize expected utility.

### Pair 253
- **question**: How does Reiter's default logic handle the expression of nonmonotonicity?
- **gold**: In Reiter's approach to default logic, nonmonotonicity is not directly expressed in the language itself—the language of default logic remains the same as that of first-order logic. Instead, nonmonotonicity is accommodated through a set of default rules (rules of the form DR) that can be part of a theory. Reiter provides a fixpoint definition of the extensions of theories containing such default rules, which allows the system to capture nonmonotonic reasoning while maintaining a classical logical language. ### Pair 263

The passage notes that while the frame problem has been widely studied and debated across various philosophical traditions, it remains an important issue for AI research specifically concerned with reasoning about actions.
